<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q092"></a>
## Q92. Meaning of judicial review
**Primary route:** Polity → [Fundamental Rights](../../topics/polity/fundamental-rights.md) → Meaning of judicial review  
**Primary classification:** Polity → Fundamental Rights → Meaning of judicial review  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q92 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Fundamental-Rights.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Fundamental-Rights.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `polity-meaning-of-judicial-review`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

In India, Judicial Review implies

(a) the power of the Judiciary to pronounce upon the constitutionality of laws and executive orders.  
(b) the power of the Judiciary to question the wisdom of the laws enacted by the Legislatures.  
(c) the power of the Judiciary to review all the legislative enactments before they are assented to by the President.  
(d) the power of the Judiciary to review its own judgements given earlier in similar or different cases.  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — the power of the Judiciary to pronounce upon the constitutionality of laws and executive orders.**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

In India, Judicial Review is the power of the judiciary to examine the constitutionality of legislative enactments and executive orders of both the Central and State governments. If these actions are found to be in violation of the Constitution (ultra vires), the judiciary can declare them illegal, unconstitutional, and invalid (null and void). This principle is a basic feature of the Indian Constitution, as established in the Kesavananda Bharati case (1973). While the term 'Judicial Review' is not explicitly mentioned in the Constitution, the power is derived from several provisions, most notably Article 13 (which declares laws inconsistent with Fundamental Rights as void), Article 32 (Supreme Court's writ jurisdiction), and Article 226 (High Courts' writ jurisdiction). Option 1 is incorrect because the judiciary does not question the 'wisdom' or policy merits of the legislature, only the legality. Option 2 is incorrect because the judiciary reviews laws after they are enacted, not before Presidential assent. Option 3 refers to the 'Review Jurisdiction' under Article 137, which is distinct from the general concept of Judicial Review of legislative and executive actions (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 27; NCERT Class 11, Indian Constitution at Work, Chapter 6).

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: the power of the Judiciary to pronounce upon the constitutionality of laws and executive orders.** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **B: the power of the Judiciary to question the wisdom of the laws enacted by the Legislatures.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: the power of the Judiciary to review all the legislative enactments before they are assented to by the President.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: the power of the Judiciary to review its own judgements given earlier in similar or different cases.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **meaning of judicial review**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.  
**UPSC trap:** The distractors exploit close institutional names, absolute qualifiers, chronology, category errors, or one partly true statement embedded in a wrong combination.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q093"></a>
## Q93. RIN Mutiny, Quit India Movement and Second Round Table Conference chronology
**Primary route:** Modern History → [Post-War Upsurge and Quit India](../../topics/modern-history/post-war-upsurge-ina-rin-mutiny-and-cabinet-mission.md) → RIN Mutiny, Quit India Movement and Second Round Table Conference chronology  
**Primary classification:** Modern History → Post-War Upsurge and Quit India → RIN Mutiny, Quit India Movement and Second Round Table Conference chronology  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q93 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Modern-Indian-History/basic/26_Post-War-Upsurge-INA-RIN-Mutiny-Cabinet-Mission.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Modern-Indian-History/basic/26_Post-War-Upsurge-INA-RIN-Mutiny-Cabinet-Mission.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `modern-history-rin-mutiny-quit-india-movement-and-second-round-table-conference-chronology`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

With reference to Indian freedom struggle, consider the following events:
1. Mutiny in Royal Indian Navy
2. Quit India Movement launched
3. Second Round Table Conference

What is the correct chronological sequence of the above events?

(a) 1-2-3  
(b) 2-1-3  
(c) 3-2-1  
(d) 3-1-2  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 3-2-1**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The correct chronological sequence of the events is as follows: 
1. **Second Round Table Conference (September–December 1931):** This conference was held in London. It is significant because it was the only Round Table Conference attended by Mahatma Gandhi, who represented the Indian National Congress following the Gandhi-Irwin Pact (NCERT Class 12, Themes in Indian History Part III, Chapter 13).
2. **Quit India Movement (August 1942):** Launched on August 8, 1942, at the Gowalia Tank Maidan in Bombay. Mahatma Gandhi gave the famous 'Do or Die' speech, demanding an immediate end to British rule in India (NCERT Class 12, Themes in Indian History Part III, Chapter 13).
3. **Mutiny in Royal Indian Navy (February 1946):** Also known as the RIN Revolt or the Bombay Mutiny, it began on February 18, 1946, on the HMIS Talwar. The ratings (sailors) protested against racial discrimination, unpalatable food, and the trial of the Indian National Army (INA) officers (Bipan Chandra, India's Struggle for Independence, Chapter 39).
Thus, the sequence is 3 (1931) -> 2 (1942) -> 1 (1946), which corresponds to option index 2.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **1: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **2: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **3: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1-2-3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: 2-1-3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: 3-2-1** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **D: 3-1-2** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **rIN Mutiny, Quit India Movement and Second Round Table Conference chronology**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q094"></a>
## Q94. Tax revenue and fiscal deficit trends
**Primary route:** Economy → [Union Budget, Fiscal Policy and Deficits](../../topics/economy/union-budget-fiscal-policy-and-deficit-indicators.md) → Tax revenue and fiscal deficit trends  
**Primary classification:** Economy → Union Budget, Fiscal Policy and Deficits → Tax revenue and fiscal deficit trends  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q94 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Economy/basic/09_Union-Budget-Fiscal-Policy-and-Deficit-Indicators.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Economy/basic/09_Union-Budget-Fiscal-Policy-and-Deficit-Indicators.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `economy-tax-revenue-and-fiscal-deficit-trends`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following statements:
1. Tax revenue as a percent of GDP of India has steadily increased in the last decade.
2. Fiscal deficit as a percent of GDP of India has steadily increased in the last decade.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 only  
(c) Both 1 and 2  
(d) Neither 1 nor 2  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — Neither 1 nor 2**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The question, from the UPSC Civil Services Preliminary Examination 2017, asks about economic trends in India over the 'last decade' (approximately 2007–2017). 

1. **Statement 1 is incorrect**: Tax revenue as a percentage of GDP did not increase 'steadily' (meaning every year without a dip). According to the Economic Survey and RBI data, the tax-to-GDP ratio reached a peak of approximately 12.1% in 2007-08. However, it declined significantly to around 9.6% in 2009-10 following the global financial crisis and subsequent economic slowdown. While it recovered in later years (reaching around 11.3% by 2016-17), the trend was fluctuating rather than steadily increasing. 

2. **Statement 2 is incorrect**: Fiscal deficit as a percentage of GDP also did not steadily increase. In fact, it followed a volatile path. It was relatively low in 2007-08 (around 2.5%) but spiked to 6.0% in 2008-09 and 6.5% in 2009-10 due to the government's fiscal stimulus packages. From 2011-12 onwards, the government generally followed a path of fiscal consolidation, with the deficit gradually declining to 3.5% by 2016-17. 

Since both statements are incorrect, the correct option is 'Neither 1 nor 2'.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **1: Incorrect.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **2: Incorrect.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: 2 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: Both 1 and 2** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: Neither 1 nor 2** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **tax revenue and fiscal deficit trends**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.  
**UPSC trap:** The distractors exploit close institutional names, absolute qualifiers, chronology, category errors, or one partly true statement embedded in a wrong combination.

## Q95. Lion translocation to Kuno Palpur
**Primary route:** Environment and Ecology → [Protected Area Network in India](../../topics/environment/protected-area-network-india.md) → Lion translocation to Kuno Palpur  
**Primary classification:** Environment and Ecology → Protected Area Network in India → Lion translocation to Kuno Palpur  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q95 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/06_Protected-Area-Network-India.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/06_Protected-Area-Network-India.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `environment-and-ecology-lion-translocation-to-kuno-palpur`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Recently there was a proposal to translocate some of the lions from their natural habitat in Gujarat to which one of the following sites?

(a) Corbett National Park  
(b) Kuno Palpur Wildlife Sanctuary  
(c) Mudumalai Wildlife Sanctuary  
(d) Sariska National Park  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — Kuno Palpur Wildlife Sanctuary**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The proposal to translocate Asiatic lions (Panthera leo leo) from their only natural habitat in Gir National Park, Gujarat, to a second location was initiated to protect the species from potential extinction due to epidemics, large-scale forest fires, or other natural calamities. The Kuno Palpur Wildlife Sanctuary (now Kuno National Park) in Madhya Pradesh was identified as the most suitable site for this purpose. In a landmark judgment on April 15, 2013, the Supreme Court of India (Centre for Environmental Law, WWF-I vs. Union of India & Others) directed the Ministry of Environment and Forests to facilitate the translocation of Asiatic lions to Kuno within six months. The court emphasized that the preservation of the species is a top priority over state claims. While the lion translocation has faced administrative delays, Kuno Palpur later became the site for the world's first intercontinental large wild carnivore translocation project with the introduction of Cheetahs in 2022.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Corbett National Park** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: Kuno Palpur Wildlife Sanctuary** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **C: Mudumalai Wildlife Sanctuary** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: Sariska National Park** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **lion translocation to Kuno Palpur**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q096"></a>
## Q96. Consequences of President's Rule
**Primary route:** Polity → [Emergency Provisions](../../topics/polity/emergency-provisions.md) → Consequences of President's Rule  
**Primary classification:** Polity → Emergency Provisions → Consequences of President's Rule  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q96 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Emergency-Provisions.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Emergency-Provisions.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `polity-consequences-of-president-s-rule`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following are not necessarily the consequences of the proclamation of the President's rule in a State?
1. Dissolution of the State Legislative Assembly
2. Removal of the Council of Ministers in the State
3. Dissolution of the local bodies

Select the correct answer using the code given below:

(a) 1 and 2 only  
(b) 1 and 3 only  
(c) 2 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — 1 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

When the President's Rule is proclaimed in a State under Article 356 of the Indian Constitution, the following consequences occur:

1. **Removal of the Council of Ministers (Statement 2):** This is a **necessary** consequence. The President dismisses the State Council of Ministers headed by the Chief Minister. The Governor, on behalf of the President, carries on the state administration with the help of the Chief Secretary or advisors appointed by the President (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter on Emergency Provisions).

2. **Dissolution of the State Legislative Assembly (Statement 1):** This is **not necessarily** a consequence. The President has the power to either suspend or dissolve the State Legislative Assembly. In the landmark *S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994)* case, the Supreme Court ruled that the Assembly should not be dissolved until the proclamation has been approved by both Houses of Parliament. Until such approval, the President can only keep the Assembly in 'suspended animation' (D.D. Basu, Introduction to the Constitution of India).

3. **Dissolution of Local Bodies (Statement 3):** This is **not** a consequence of President's Rule. The proclamation of President's Rule under Article 356 deals with the failure of the constitutional machinery at the State level (Executive and Legislature). It does not affect the tenure or functioning of local self-government bodies like Panchayats and Municipalities, which are governed by Parts IX and IXA of the Constitution and respective State laws.

Since statements 1 and 3 are not necessarily the consequences, the correct option is 1 and 3 only.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **1: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **2: Incorrect.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **3: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 and 2 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: 1 and 3 only** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **C: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **consequences of President's Rule**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.  
**UPSC trap:** The distractors exploit close institutional names, absolute qualifiers, chronology, category errors, or one partly true statement embedded in a wrong combination.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q097"></a>
## Q97. Right against Exploitation
**Primary route:** Polity → [Fundamental Rights](../../topics/polity/fundamental-rights.md) → Right against Exploitation  
**Primary classification:** Polity → Fundamental Rights → Right against Exploitation  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q97 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Fundamental-Rights.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Fundamental-Rights.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `polity-right-against-exploitation`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following are envisaged by the Right against Exploitation in the Constitution of India?
1. Prohibition of traffic in human beings and forced labour
2. Abolition of untouchability
3. Protection of the interests of minorities
4. Prohibition of employment of children in factories and mines

Select the correct answer using the code given below:

(a) 1, 2 and 4 only  
(b) 2, 3 and 4 only  
(c) 1 and 4 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 1 and 4 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The 'Right against Exploitation' is a Fundamental Right enshrined in Articles 23 and 24 of the Constitution of India. 

1. **Statement 1 is correct**: Article 23 prohibits 'traffic in human beings and begar and other similar forms of forced labour'. It protects individuals against exploitation by both the State and private persons (Constitution of India, Art. 23).
2. **Statement 2 is incorrect**: The abolition of untouchability is provided under Article 17, which falls under the 'Right to Equality' (Articles 14–18), not the Right against Exploitation (Constitution of India, Art. 17).
3. **Statement 3 is incorrect**: The protection of the interests of minorities is provided under Article 29, which falls under 'Cultural and Educational Rights' (Articles 29–30) (Constitution of India, Art. 29).
4. **Statement 4 is correct**: Article 24 prohibits the employment of children below the age of 14 years in any factory, mine, or other hazardous employment (Constitution of India, Art. 24).

Therefore, only statements 1 and 4 are envisaged by the Right against Exploitation.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **1: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **2: Incorrect.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **3: Incorrect.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.
- **4: Correct.** Read with the reasoned explanation above and the official keyed combination.

## Q98. Great Nicobar's nearest island
**Primary route:** Geography → [World Regional Geography](../../topics/geography/world-regional-geography-continents-countries.md) → Great Nicobar's nearest island  
**Primary classification:** Geography → World Regional Geography → Great Nicobar's nearest island

**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following is geographically closest to Great Nicobar?

(a) Sumatra  
(b) Borneo  
(c) Java  
(d) Sri Lanka  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — Sumatra**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Great Nicobar is the southernmost island of the Andaman and Nicobar archipelago and contains Indira Point, the southernmost point of India. Geographically, Great Nicobar is separated from the Indonesian island of Sumatra by the Great Channel, also known as the Six Degree Channel. The distance between the southern tip of Great Nicobar and the northern tip of Sumatra (specifically the Aceh province) is approximately 145-150 km (NCERT Class 11 Geography, Chapter 2). In comparison, Sri Lanka is located approximately 1,200 km to the west, Java is about 1,800 km to the southeast, and Borneo is more than 2,400 km away. This makes Sumatra the closest geographical neighbor among the provided options (UPSC GS Paper 1, 2017).

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Sumatra** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **B: Borneo** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: Java** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: Sri Lanka** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **great Nicobar's nearest island**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q099"></a>
## Q99. Principle of Cabinet government
**Primary route:** Polity → [Parliamentary System](../../topics/polity/prime-minister-and-council-of-ministers.md) → Principle of Cabinet government  
**Primary classification:** Polity → Parliamentary System → Principle of Cabinet government  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q99 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Parliamentary-System.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Parliamentary-System.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `polity-principle-of-cabinet-government`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Out of the following statements, choose the one that brings out the principle underlying the Cabinet form of Government:

(a) An arrangement for minimizing the criticism against the Government whose responsibilities are complex and hard to carry out to the satisfaction of all.  
(b) A mechanism for speeding up the activities of the Government whose responsibilities are increasing day by day.  
(c) A mechanism of parliamentary democracy for ensuring collective responsibility of the Government to the people.  
(d) A device for strengthening the hands of the head of the Government whose hold over the people is in a state of decline.  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — A mechanism of parliamentary democracy for ensuring collective responsibility of the Government to the people.**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Cabinet form of government, also known as the Parliamentary system, is fundamentally based on the principle of 'Collective Responsibility'. In India, this principle is explicitly enshrined in Article 75(3) of the Constitution, which states that the Council of Ministers shall be collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha (House of the People). This means that the executive (the Cabinet) is accountable to the legislature, and by extension, to the people who elect the members of the legislature. Under this principle, all ministers are jointly responsible for every decision made by the Cabinet; they 'swim or sink together'. If the Lok Sabha passes a vote of no-confidence against the Council of Ministers, the entire government must resign, ensuring that the executive remains responsive to the will of the people's representatives. While other options like efficiency (Option 1) or managing criticism (Option 0) might be outcomes or features of a government, they do not constitute the foundational constitutional principle of the Cabinet system.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: An arrangement for minimizing the criticism against the Government whose responsibilities are complex and hard to carry out to the satisfaction of all.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: A mechanism for speeding up the activities of the Government whose responsibilities are increasing day by day.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: A mechanism of parliamentary democracy for ensuring collective responsibility of the Government to the people.** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.
- **D: A device for strengthening the hands of the head of the Government whose hold over the people is in a state of decline.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **principle of Cabinet government**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.  
**UPSC trap:** The distractors exploit close institutional names, absolute qualifiers, chronology, category errors, or one partly true statement embedded in a wrong combination.

<a id="2017-prelims-gs1-q100"></a>
## Q100. Non-feature of Indian federalism
**Primary route:** Polity → [Federal System](../../topics/polity/federal-system.md) → Non-feature of Indian federalism  
**Primary classification:** Polity → Federal System → Non-feature of Indian federalism  
**Metadata:** 2017 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q100 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination  
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Federal-System.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Federal-System.md)  
**Canonical slug:** `polity-non-feature-of-indian-federalism`  
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which one of the following is not a feature of Indian federalism?

(a) There is an independent judiciary in India.  
(b) Powers have been clearly divided between the Centre and the States.  
(c) The federating units have been given unequal representation in the Rajya Sabha.  
(d) It is the result of an agreement among the federating units.  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — It is the result of an agreement among the federating units.**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Indian federalism is unique and is often described as 'quasi-federal' or a 'Union of States' rather than a 'Federation of States'. According to Dr. B.R. Ambedkar, the Indian federation is not the result of an agreement by the units (states), and the units have no right to secede from it (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 13: Federal System). This distinguishes it from the American federation, which is a result of an agreement between independent states. 

Regarding the other options:
- Option 0: An independent judiciary is a fundamental feature of the Indian Constitution to settle disputes between the Centre and States (NCERT Class 11, Indian Constitution at Work, Chapter 7).
- Option 1: The Seventh Schedule of the Constitution clearly demarcates powers through the Union List, State List, and Concurrent List (Article 246).
- Option 2: Unlike the US Senate where all states have equal representation, the Rajya Sabha in India provides representation based on the population of each state, leading to unequal representation as per the Fourth Schedule (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 13).

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: There is an independent judiciary in India.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **B: Powers have been clearly divided between the Centre and the States.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **C: The federating units have been given unequal representation in the Rajya Sabha.** — Incorrect: it conflicts with at least one verified proposition or with the controlling official-final combination explained above.
- **D: It is the result of an agreement among the federating units.** — Correct: this is the proposition/combination fixed by the UPSC official-final Series-A key and supported by the explanation.

**Elimination logic:** Identify the decisive distinction in **non-feature of Indian federalism**, reject options containing a demonstrably false statement or category mismatch, and then map the surviving set to A-D. Elimination corroborates evidence; it does not replace it.  
**UPSC trap:** The distractors exploit close institutional names, absolute qualifiers, chronology, category errors, or one partly true statement embedded in a wrong combination.
